[TABLE]
*Level 1 (Strategy) Level 2 (Approach) HPSGCN SGCN Total A.1 Direct Habitat Management Develop and maintain current monitoring data on SGCN abundance, distribution, and condition 6. Foster research to improve knowledge regarding SGCN populations and habitats 7. Effectively communicate with all audiences regarding conservation of SGCN to inform the public and engage the conservation community 8. Continue to integrate conservation of SGCN into the many forms of natural resource planning Action: Use the Open Space Plan to designate important SGCN habitats for priority acquisition. Measure: Number of acres protected. Action: Reduce negative impacts to SGCN from the intake of cooling water and the discharge of heated water at electric generation facilities. Measure: All facilities have implemented measures to reduce impingement and entrainment of SGCN at water intakes and the discharge of heated water. Action: Reduce negative impacts of altered flow in streams and rivers. Measure: Number of permits modified or issued that ensure adequate flow. Action: Adopt point-source discharge permit limits that reduce impacts to SGCN at key locations. Measure: Reduction in discharge permit limits at key facilities. Objective: Reduce the negative impacts to SGCN from atmospheric deposition of mercury and acid. Action: Continue controlling mercury discharges from energy-generating and industrial facilities. Measure: Decline in mercury levels in impacted SGCN. Action: Continue policies to reduce greenhouse gas emission in New York by 40 percent by 2030 and 80 percent by 2050. Measure: Total carbon dioxide emissions in the New York Greenhouse Gas Inventory. Action: Minimize negative impacts to wildlife from state energy policies to reduce emissions, such as large-scale renewable power generation. Measure: Integration of up-to-date research on impacts to wildlife and potential mitigation actions into environmental reviews. Action: Control double-crested cormorants, gulls, and problematic mammalian predators at locations where they are negatively impacting nesting birds or other SGCN. Measure: Nesting success of SGCN species at managed locations. Action: Continue development of rapid response mechanisms and long-term strategies to control new occurrences of terrestrial invasive species. Measure: An invasive species strategic plan, including rapid response protocols, is completed. Action: Require development projects to have a plan to ensure invasive species are not introduced to or spread around the project site. Measure: Number of projects successful in maintaining target or baseline levels of invasive species after project completion. Objective: Manage natural succession to maintain the diversity of vegetative systems and native forest types and age classes to support self-sustaining populations of the full array of native terrestrial wildlife. Action: Encourage private forest owners to manage their forest lands for the benefit of SGCN dependent on young forests. Measure: Acres of private forest land managed for SGCN dependent on young forest habitat.*

* The Long Island National Wildlife Refuge Complex is comprised of multiple units and protects approximately 6,500 acres of important marine, coastal, and upland habitats used by SGCN. Monitoring in NWRs provides a baseline from which to measure change as well as detailed monitoring of select species and habitats.The New York Natural Heritage Program (NYNHP) tracks the locations of several animal SGCN across the state, though regular monitoring for most species and locations is not done. NYNHP also monitors populations of plant SGCN on State Forest lands in most of the state (excluding Long Island and New York City). This monitoring of plants is done on a five-year rotation and conducted in cooperation with DEC's Division of Lands and Forests' Unit Management Plans. This monitoring does not include DEC lands in the Catskill or Adirondack Forest Preserves, or on any other lands besides State Forests. NYNHP, in collaboration with the New York Office of Parks, Recreation and Historic Preservation (OPRHP), also monitors rare plant populations occurring on State Park lands owned by OPRHP, on a ten-year rotation.Appendix 5 contains a list of monitoring programs in New York coordinated and implemented by DEC and partners. These new, recently completed, and ongoing programs provide fundamental information on many SGCN and are the core of current species and habitat monitoring efforts in New York. However, these monitoring programs often originate from specific information needs and collectively do not form the type of unified program that can lead to greater efficiencies in monitoring effort.Habitat MonitoringHabitat parameters are frequently recorded as a component of other projects, but there is no ongoing, systematic monitoring of all habitat types in New York. Monitoring programs are typically focused on program goals and are not focused specifically on SGCN. For aquatic habitats, monitoring is mostly related to environmental quality and often measures impairment of human use rather than evaluating the functionality of natural systems. The environmental monitoring conducted by the Bureau of Ecosystem Health, in DEC's Division of Fish and Wildlife, measures levels of chemical contamination, determines contaminant uptake and effects in biota, and continues monitoring after the restoration of contaminated sites. The Forest Health Research Lab, in DEC's Division of Lands and Forests, monitors various aspects of New York's forests, including the impacts of drastic or sudden vegetation cover changes from various causes (e.g., southern pine beetle, oak wilt, fire, etc.), and the resulting changes to the forest community and wildlife species. Forest habitat monitoring is conducted by the US Department of Agriculture through the Forest Inventory and Analysis Program, but there are few comprehensive monitoring programs for non-forested terrestrial habitats.The NYNHP monitors exemplary Natural Communities ranked S1 or S2 and located in State Forests (excluding Long Island, New York City, and Forest Preserve lands). This Natural Community monitoring is conducted on a five-year rotation, in coordination with the Division of Lands and Forests' Unit Management Plans. The Adirondack Mountain Club monitors alpine summit natural communities, and the plants therein, of the Adirondack High Peaks approximately every six years.Currently, numerous monitoring programs are conducted throughout New York, recording important information for species and habitats. The data collected by these monitoring projects are analyzed and archived by DEC staff or conservation partner organizations and is made available upon request to other agencies, researchers, or the public.*

| invertebrates. |

| whale SGCN, Between 2022-2025, DEC hired several staff to support the development and |
| implementation of conservation projects for reptiles, amphibians, mammals, birds, freshwater fish and |
| mussels. |

*Table 7 The Nature Conservancy assessed the current conditions of habitats in the Northeast. The final report, Conservation Status of Natural Habitats in the Northeast(Anderson et al. 2023) provides baseline measures at the regional level from which to gauge progress on conservation efforts. Refining these data and using results to evaluate conservation progress at the state level is an ongoing effort that supports updating the SWAP and evaluation of other conservation efforts.Building upon the effort to design performance measures at the northeast regional level, the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies' Teaming with Wildlife Committee formed an Effectiveness Measures Workgroup to develop a framework that can be used by states to improve performance reporting. The workgroup's report, Measuring the Effectiveness of State Wildlife Grants (AFWA 2011), recommends indicators to measure status and trends of natural resources, as well as effectiveness of conservation actions supported by SWG. The report provides templates for reporting results of conservation actions and methods to incorporate this information into grant reporting and adaptive management. Effectiveness measures for conservation actions are shown in Table7-2.*

* New York City is an important partner in implementing the SWAP. In addition to managing wildlife and habitat within the city, the New York City Department of Environmental Protection owns and manages tens of thousands of acres of land, primarily forests surrounding their water supply reservoirs in the Catskills and lower Hudson Valley. These lands are protected from development and maintained in natural vegetative cover, providing habitat for dozens of SGCN.Numerous local governments and agencies will have a part in implementation of the SWAP. County and town governments in New York have used the SWAP in developing local conservation plans, and in planning for recreational use of their parks and open space. County Soil and Water Conservation Districts regularly work with DEC staff and other conservation professionals on projects that benefit SGCN.In addition to these state-and national-level organizations, there are many local clubs and organizations that also contribute to implementation. These smaller groups help disseminate environmental information, and often have monitoring projects that collect valuable data on local conditions.Universities conduct many projects that contribute to SWAP implementation, especially Cornell University, and multiple campuses of the State University of New York, including Albany, Brockport, Buffalo, Cobleskill, Environmental Science and Forestry, New Paltz, Oswego, Plattsburgh, and Potsdam.Research actions to better understand the life history and population status of SGCN are conducted by not-for-profit organizations and the academic community. Implementation of these actions somewhat depends upon available funding and staff with particular expertise, so it is not possible to assign the needed research to any particular university or organization. However, research partners collaborate to make continuous progress on research that fills in data gaps regarding species, habitats, and threats.Outreach and communication of natural resource conservation issues is a cross-cutting goal that strengthens a public conservation ethic for protection and management. DEC and conservation partners have many ongoing outreach programs, including classroom presentations, teacher training, media communications, publication of non-technical reports, and volunteer stewardship projects. Volunteer Stewardship Agreements are increasingly used to implement conservation projects. Community Science is used to conduct avian monitoring, such as with the Third New York Breeding Bird Atlas, Winter Raptor Surveys, Mountain Birdwatch, and Hawk Watches. Volunteers also contribute to data collection on other taxa through participating in FrogWatch, the Amphibian Migrations and Road Crossing Project, monitoring juvenile glass eels in the Hudson River, odonate surveys, pollinator surveys, bat acoustic monitoring surveys, and monitoring monarch butterfly migration. Platforms such as iNaturalist, eBird, and iMapInvasives allow the public to submit observations of plants and animals. Those data are used for a variety of monitoring and research projects. There is potential to increase volunteer science and stewardship projects to gather data on species abundance, distribution, habitat characteristics, and to record phenology of seasonal events. All of these are important data for assessing the impacts of climate change on SGCN.Projects to survey, monitor, or research SGCN that involve direct handling of animals require a DEC Special License or Temporary Revocable Permit, most commonly a License to Collect, Possess or Sell. This license authorizes the holder to possess wildlife for education, exhibition, propagation, bird banding, and other scientific purposes, and requires the holder to submit annual reports documenting activities carried out under the license. These reports provide information on where in the state some conservation work is conducted and the status of those projects. Another Special License that is relevant to conservation of SGCN is a Wildlife Rehabilitator License, which allows for the temporary housing, treatment, and release of injured wildlife. The Wildlife Rehabilitation Council provides training and support for rehabilitating injured wildlife in New York. Wildlife rehabilitators are often also engaged in education programs, presenting information on wildlife conservation to school and community groups, using live, non-releasable animals. Wildlife rehabilitators are also frequently the first point of professional contact for diseased wildlife, and they work closely with the Wildlife Health Program to implement the Wildlife Health Program Strategic Plan (NYSDEC 2021), The Wildlife Health Program is a partnership between DEC and Cornell University's College of Veterinary Medicine Animal Health Diagnostic Center.*

## Threat Characteristics

Three threat characteristics can be used to help prioritize actions by identifying the most pervasive, severe and urgent threats to species, species groups, and/or habitats. Level 2 threats were identified 1,665 times for HPSGCN and 2,248 times for SGCN, though not all had threat characteristics quantified (see individual species status assessments). Identified as Spatial Extent (Scope), Severity, and Immediacy, these characteristics are further defined below.
Spatial Extent (Scope): Consider the impact of the threat within the next 10 years in New York.
• Small (low impact) = A small portion of the habitat or population is negatively impacted by the threat (<10%)
• Restricted (moderate impact) = (10-30%)
• Large (moderate impact) = (30-70%)
• Pervasive (high impact) = A large portion of the habitat or population is negatively impacted by the threat (>70%)
Severity: Impact of threat within the spatial extent within New York over the next 10 years.
• Slight (low impact) = degree of ecological change is minor (reduce population 1-10%)
• Moderate (moderate impact) = (reduce population by 11-30%)
• Serious (moderate impact) = (reduce population by 31-70%)
• Extreme (high impact) = Likely to destroy or eliminate occurrence or reduce population by 71-100%

## Immediacy:

The timescale over which impacts of the threat will be observable in New York.
• Long-term (low impact) = Effects of the threat are expected in 10-100 years given known ecosystem interactions or compounding threats
• Near-term (moderate impact) = Effects of the threat are expected within the next 1-10 years 

## Freshwater Aquatic Species and Habitats

## Statewide Priority Actions

• Remove or replace high-priority barriers that impede aquatic organism passage.
• Continue SGCN fish surveys.
• Monitor chemical contaminants in fish.
• Continue and expand freshwater mussel surveys.
• Continue to develop and implement non-invasive sampling techniques, such as eDNA Draft New York State Wildlife Action Plan (July 2025)

## Lower Hudson/Long Island

• Monitor population and assess spawning habitat of banded sunfish.
• Continue programs to restore and monitor populations of American shad.
• Manage submerged aquatic vegetation to maintain communities dominated by natural vegetation.
• Continue to monitor Atlantic sturgeon population, spawning, recruitment, and habitat use.
• Continue to monitor shortnose sturgeon population.
• Survey for presence of tidewater mucket mussel.
• Remove barriers to the migration of alewife and American eel.

## Upper Hudson/Mohawk

• Continue round whitefish restoration efforts.

## Delaware

• Continue to evaluate American eel population, life history, and harvest.
• Survey extant populations and restore historic habitat of comely shiner.
• Survey for presence of ironcolor shiner.
• Assess swallowtail shiner population and habitat.
• Continue programs to restore and monitor populations of American shad.
• Survey for presence of dwarf wedgemussel.

## Susquehanna

• Restore aquatic habitat connectivity for American eel migration.
• Survey extant populations and restore historic habitat of comely shiner.
• Assess swallowtail shiner population and habitat.
• Continue eastern hellbender headstarting project.
• Survey for presence of green floater mussel.
• Continue Eastern Brook Trout Joint Venture.
• Protect habitats of freshwater mussel SGCN.

## Allegheny

• Inventory bluebreast darter population and habitat.
• Evaluate success of gilt darter restoration and continue trap and transfer as necessary.
• Continue paddlefish population restoration.
• Survey population and protect habitat of river redhorse.
• Restore sauger and monitor population.
• Survey spotted darter population and protect spawning habitats.
• Continue eastern hellbender headstarting project.
• Protect habitats of freshwater mussel SGCN.
